  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F20, F31\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... I just knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. And I have done so much. I mean, I organized the Easter luncheon for six years straight! I made my famous potato salad—the secret is a little bit of fresh dill, you know. My mother taught me that. She always grew her own dill in this little window box in the kitchen. It faced south, so it got the absolute best morning light. The tomatoes didn't do quite as well, though, because the neighbor's big oak tree blocked the sun by noon. That tree must have been a hundred years old. The city finally cut it down last year, which was a shame, really, all that history just gone through a woodchipper in an afternoon. Makes a terrible racket, too. But... wait, what was I saying? Oh. Yes. The committee. They're trying to erase me.\n\nDoctor: When you say the parish committee is trying to erase you, can you explain exactly what you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Well, it's just like I said, they aren't just arguing with me anymore. It's a whole... a whole orchestrated thing. My in-laws, they're using the altar, the actual altar, and the parish committee to push me out completely. They want to make everyone in the church and the family forget what I have done for them. And I've done so much. Like the time I helped my sister-in-law pick out her wedding dress. We went to this little boutique downtown, the one with the pink awning? They served us champagne in these beautiful crystal flutes. I still have one of them, actually. I kept it as a souvenir. It sits on my mantle right next to the porcelain dog my husband won for me at the state fair in... oh, it must have been 1985. We ate so much cotton candy that day I thought I'd be sick on the Ferris wheel. The lights on that wheel were just spectacular against the night sky, though. You could see them from miles away, just spinning and spinning. But yes, the committee. They're definitely working with my in-laws to erase my whole legacy in this family.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that others might not see things the way you do, or that there could be another explanation for what's happening with your in-laws and the parish committee?\n\nPatient: Another explanation? Well... no, because I can see right through them. Other people might be blind to it, but like I was saying earlier, I know for a fact this isn't just a regular argument. I came in today because I know they are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget everything I've done for this family. \n\nAnd the altar... you know, the wood on that altar is solid mahogany. Father Thomas had it shipped all the way from somewhere in South America, I think Brazil? Or maybe Peru. I had a friend who went to Peru once, Brenda. She brought me back this lovely little woven blanket. It's so soft, made of real alpaca wool. Alpacas are such funny-looking creatures, aren't they? With those long necks and big eyes. They look like they're always judging you. Kind of like my mother-in-law, actually, always looking down her nose when I serve store-bought dinner rolls instead of homemade. But who has the time to wait for yeast to rise when it's so humid out? The humidity just ruins my hair anyway. It gets all frizzy right at the roots, no matter how much hairspray I use. I used to use Aqua Net, the one in the pink can, but they changed the formula and it just isn't the same anymore. \n\nWhat did you ask me? Oh, about other people seeing it differently. No, they just don't see the lengths my in-laws are going to. I know what they're doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your belief about your in-laws and the parish committee has been challenged by others who don't see the same pattern that you do, and if so, how do you respond to their different perspective?\n\nPatient: Well, people try to challenge me, sure. My sister tried just the other day. But like I've been saying since I walked in here, this isn't just some silly argument with my in-laws. I know for a fact they are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget every single thing I have done for this family! So when people like my sister tell me I'm seeing patterns that aren't there, I just hang up the phone. \n\nI hung up on her last Tuesday. Or was it Wednesday? It must have been Wednesday because I was watching my daytime stories. I've been watching the same soap opera for thirty years. Although, the storylines lately are just ridiculous. They brought back a character who supposedly died in a plane crash ten years ago! He just walked right into the hospital like nothing happened. I mean, who writes this stuff? My husband used to say those shows would rot my brain, but he would watch football all Sunday, jumping up and yelling at the TV screen like the coach could actually hear him. He spilled a whole bowl of salsa on the living room rug once doing that. I had to scrub it with club soda and baking soda for an hour. Baking soda really is a miracle worker, you know. You can use it for absolutely anything—cleaning, baking, even keeping the fridge smelling fresh. I always keep an open box in the back next to the milk. You have to change it out every thirty days, though, or it stops absorbing the odors. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you ask about my sister?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me whether you have ever questioned your belief about your in-laws and the parish committee, or if you have ever considered that it might not be true?\n\nPatient: Question it? Why would I question it? I came in here today specifically because I knew, I absolutely knew, that my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget everything I have done for this family! It's as clear as day. \n\nJust like the weather was the day of the church picnic last summer. Oh, it was so sunny, not a cloud in the sky. We had it out by the lake, the one with the little wooden docks? I made my famous potato salad. I always use red potatoes because they hold their shape better when you boil them. And a little dash of paprika on top, just for color. My grandmother taught me that trick. She used to grow her own paprika peppers in her garden. Well, I think they were paprika peppers. They were little and red. She had the most beautiful garden, full of roses and hydrangeas. Hydrangeas are so temperamental, though. If the soil is too acidic, they turn blue, and if it's too alkaline, they turn pink. I prefer the blue ones, personally. They remind me of the ocean. I went to the beach once in Florida, Clearwater Beach. The sand was so soft, like powdered sugar. You could walk for miles and it wouldn't hurt your feet at all. \n\nSo no, I haven't ever considered that it might not be true. Why would I?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood was unusually high, irritable, or you felt extremely energetic for at least a week, where you were doing things that were out of character for you?\n\nPatient: High or irritable? Well, yes, I suppose. There were days recently when I felt so bright and... just completely certain about everything. Like I was running on pure electricity. But then, if anyone questioned me about the altar, I would just snap at them like they were insulting my whole family! Just bite their heads right off. \n\nSpeaking of family, my brother-in-law Larry has the absolute worst temper. He got into a screaming match with an umpire at my nephew's Little League game once. Over a strike call! Little League is just too competitive these days. When my son was young, they just played for fun, and we moms would bring orange slices for the dugout. Or wait, do they do orange slices in baseball? Maybe that was soccer. I always got the sports mixed up. But I do know baseball has nine innings, because of the seventh-inning stretch. They always play that song, and people buy peanuts and Cracker Jacks. I never liked peanuts, though. The little skins get stuck right between your teeth, and if you don't have floss handy, it just drives you crazy all afternoon. I always carry a little pack of mint waxed floss in my purse. The unwaxed kind just shreds, especially around the back molars. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Did you ask if I had a mint?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up?\n\nPatient: Well, my sleep has been all over the place. At first, a few weeks ago, I was only sleeping maybe two hours a night. I'd pop right out of bed, full of energy, and start making lists of all the things the parish committee was doing wrong. But lately, it's different. Now I just lie awake in the dark, hours before dawn, just worrying and worrying, and I never feel rested anymore. \n\nI used to sleep like a rock, though. Especially when we had that old mattress, the one with the pillow top. We bought it at Sears during a Labor Day sale. Sears used to have the best sales. You could get everything there—appliances, clothes, tools. My husband bought his first Craftsman socket set there, and he swore by it. He kept it in this heavy red metal toolbox in the garage. The garage always smelled like motor oil and damp cardboard. We had so many boxes out there. I kept meaning to go through them and organize the Christmas decorations, but you know how it is. You put it off until December, and then you're scrambling to find the good ornaments. I have this one ornament, a little glass bird with a spun-glass tail, that belonged to my mother. You have to clip it onto the branch so carefully or it shatters. Glass is so fragile like that. \n\nWait, did you ask about my mother's ornaments?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when you were involved in activities at a much higher level than usual, like working on multiple projects or being overly involved in church or family affairs?\n\nPatient: Well, I did feel like I could do absolutely everything for a while there. I remember I signed up for all these extra cooking shifts for the church bake sale, and I was trying to organize this whole new group of volunteers. I even started calling up relatives I hadn't spoken to in years, just dialing them up out of the blue because I felt like I had to bring everyone together right that second! \n\nSpeaking of calling people, my old rotary phone used to have the longest cord. You could stretch it all the way from the kitchen counter into the dining room. It was that mustard yellow color, you know the one? Everyone had those appliances in the seventies. Mustard yellow and avocado green. We had an avocado green refrigerator that lasted twenty years. They just don't make things like they used to. Now everything is stainless steel and it shows every single fingerprint. If you don't use that special spray, it just looks a mess. I buy the spray in bulk at Costco. Costco is wonderful, but you go in for paper towels and you come out with a kayak and a three-pound tub of mayonnaise. Who can eat three pounds of mayonnaise before it goes bad? Unless you're making potato salad for an army, I suppose. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you need a volunteer for something?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when you were involved in activities at a much higher level than usual, like working on multiple projects or being overly involved in church or family affairs?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about the bake sale, yes, absolutely. I just could not stay still for the life of me! I was up cleaning the baseboards, calling people left and right, rewriting all the dinner menus for the month, and dragging heavy boxes of decorations around. My husband and everyone kept telling me to just sit down and rest, but I couldn't! \n\nSpeaking of those decorations, I was getting out the spring things, you know, for Easter. Easter always sneaks up on you when it's in March. I used to make my daughter wear those itchy crinoline slips under her Easter dress so the skirt would puff out. She hated them! Kids' clothes today are so much softer. Everything is that stretchy knit fabric. It's practical, I suppose, but nobody dresses up anymore. You go to church and people are wearing blue jeans! My mother would have had a fit. She never left the house without her hair set and a nice pair of sensible pumps. She bought all her shoes at a little boutique downtown that closed in the nineties. It's a coffee shop now. They charge six dollars for a cup of coffee and they pour the milk into it so it looks like a leaf. It's pretty, but who wants to drink a leaf? And six dollars! You used to be able to get a whole diner breakfast for that. Two eggs, bacon, hash browns, and toast. I always liked rye toast with the little caraway seeds. \n\nWait, what did you just ask me? Did you want to know what I had for breakfast?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when you were involved in activities at a much higher level than usual, like working on multiple projects or being overly involved in church or family affairs?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose so. I just felt... well, I truly believed I was the only one who could save our family's traditions, you know? Like, if people just listened to me, the whole community event would be absolutely perfect. I had all the answers right there in my head. \n\nIt's like when you have a recipe that's been passed down for generations. My grandmother's pound cake recipe is like that. You have to use real butter, none of that margarine nonsense. Margarine came out and everyone thought it was a miracle, but it's just oil whipped up to look like butter. It doesn't taste the same at all. I remember when they used to deliver milk in glass bottles right to the porch. The cream would rise to the top, and in the winter, if it froze, the little paper cap would pop right off. The milkman's name was Mr. Higgins. He wore a white uniform and drove a truck that you could drive standing up. I always thought that was so strange, driving while standing. What if you hit a bump? You'd go flying right out the door! Cars didn't even have seatbelts back then. We used to ride in the back of my uncle's station wagon, the one with the wood paneling on the side, facing backward and waving at the cars behind us. \n\nWait, did you want the pound cake recipe? I think I have it written down on an index card somewhere.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt unusually energetic or confident for several days in a row, where you needed very little sleep and were doing a lot more than usual, like starting new projects or being very involved in church or family activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I know you keep asking about that. There were days when I felt just so bright and certain about everything. I was working on the church altar, and I had it all perfectly planned out in my head. But then, if anyone even questioned me about the altar, I would just snap at them like they were insulting my whole family! I had zero patience for anyone interfering. \n\nSpeaking of the altar, we always used those tall beeswax candles. Real beeswax, mind you, not the cheap paraffin ones that drip everywhere and ruin the good lace linens. Getting wax out of lace is an absolute nightmare. My mother taught me to put a brown paper bag over the wax and run a hot iron over it so the paper absorbs the grease. She had one of those heavy irons, the kind you actually had to be careful with or you'd scorch the fabric right through. People don't iron much anymore. Everything is wrinkle-free this and wash-and-wear that. My husband used to wear a crisp button-down shirt every single day, and I pressed every last one of them with heavy starch. The spray starch in the blue can, Faultless, I think it was called. It smells so clean. I love the smell of clean laundry drying on a line outside in the breeze. You can't do that in my neighborhood anymore, the homeowner's association says it's an eyesore. Can you imagine? A clothesline being an eyesore! They send you a nasty letter if your grass is half an inch too high or if your mailbox is the wrong shade of brown. \n\nWait, what did you ask me just now? Did you get a letter from the association too?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, the sleep. At first, I was sleeping only a couple of hours a night, if that, and I'd still be getting up in the middle of the night to make lists. I had lists for absolutely everything. I always use those yellow legal pads for my lists, the ones with the blue lines. But you have to use a good ballpoint pen, none of those gel pens that smear everywhere. My daughter bought me a pack of gel pens once, and I tried to write a grocery list, and by the time I got to the store, my handwriting was just a smudge. I was trying to buy Roma tomatoes for a sauce. You have to use Romas, you know, because the big beefsteak ones have too much water. If you try to make a sauce with those, you'll be standing at the stove simmering it for hours just to get it to thicken up. My mother used to simmer her Sunday gravy all day. The whole house smelled like garlic and basil. We grew basil in a little pot right on the kitchen windowsill. \n\nBut anyway, it's not like that anymore. Now I just lie awake before dawn worrying about every little thing, and I never, ever feel rested. Being awake before dawn is just awful because that's when the birds start up. There's this one mockingbird outside my bedroom window that just will not quit making a racket. Mockingbirds are terribly aggressive, you know. They'll dive-bomb a cat if it gets too close to their nest. We had an orange tabby cat named Marmalade when I was a girl, and he was absolutely terrified of the birds. He used to hide under the front porch whenever the blue jays came around. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Did you want to know if I have a cat?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, absolutely certain. I know they're not just arguing with me. My in-laws, I mean. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me right out, to just erase me and make everyone forget everything I've done for this family. I can see right through it. 

Just like I can see right through that committee head, Mrs. Gable. She always wears those pastel twinsets, you know the ones with the little pearl buttons? Pearls are so delicate. You're not supposed to spray perfume near them because the alcohol eats away at the finish. My husband bought me a beautiful strand of pearls for our 25th anniversary. We went to a seafood restaurant by the water to celebrate. I had the scallops. Scallops can be so tricky to cook; if you leave them in the pan even thirty seconds too long, they turn into little rubber tires. You have to get the pan screaming hot, sear them quickly on both sides, and take them right off. We had this wonderful waiter named Paul who recommended a crisp white wine to go with it. I don't usually drink wine, it gives me a terrible headache right behind my eyes. I prefer a nice cup of Earl Grey tea in the evenings. The bergamot oil in it is supposed to be very soothing. 

Wait, did you ask me if I like tea? I think I have a tin of it in my purse.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... I just knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. And I have done so much. I mean, I organized the Easter luncheon for six years straight! I made my famous potato salad—the secret is a little bit of fresh dill, you know. My mother taught me that. She always grew her own dill in this little window box in the kitchen. It faced south, so it got the absolute best morning light. The tomatoes didn't do quite as well, though, because the neighbor's big oak tree blocked the sun by noon. That tree must have been a hundred years old. The city finally cut it down last year, which was a shame, really, all that history just gone through a woodchipper in an afternoon. Makes a terrible racket, too. But... wait, what was I saying? Oh. Yes. The committee. They're trying to erase me.\n\nDoctor: When you say the parish committee is trying to erase you, can you explain exactly what you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Well, it's just like I said, they aren't just arguing with me anymore. It's a whole... a whole orchestrated thing. My in-laws, they're using the altar, the actual altar, and the parish committee to push me out completely. They want to make everyone in the church and the family forget what I have done for them. And I've done so much. Like the time I helped my sister-in-law pick out her wedding dress. We went to this little boutique downtown, the one with the pink awning? They served us champagne in these beautiful crystal flutes. I still have one of them, actually. I kept it as a souvenir. It sits on my mantle right next to the porcelain dog my husband won for me at the state fair in... oh, it must have been 1985. We ate so much cotton candy that day I thought I'd be sick on the Ferris wheel. The lights on that wheel were just spectacular against the night sky, though. You could see them from miles away, just spinning and spinning. But yes, the committee. They're definitely working with my in-laws to erase my whole legacy in this family.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that others might not see things the way you do, or that there could be another explanation for what's happening with your in-laws and the parish committee?\n\nPatient: Another explanation? Well... no, because I can see right through them. Other people might be blind to it, but like I was saying earlier, I know for a fact this isn't just a regular argument. I came in today because I know they are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget everything I've done for this family. \n\nAnd the altar... you know, the wood on that altar is solid mahogany. Father Thomas had it shipped all the way from somewhere in South America, I think Brazil? Or maybe Peru. I had a friend who went to Peru once, Brenda. She brought me back this lovely little woven blanket. It's so soft, made of real alpaca wool. Alpacas are such funny-looking creatures, aren't they? With those long necks and big eyes. They look like they're always judging you. Kind of like my mother-in-law, actually, always looking down her nose when I serve store-bought dinner rolls instead of homemade. But who has the time to wait for yeast to rise when it's so humid out? The humidity just ruins my hair anyway. It gets all frizzy right at the roots, no matter how much hairspray I use. I used to use Aqua Net, the one in the pink can, but they changed the formula and it just isn't the same anymore. \n\nWhat did you ask me? Oh, about other people seeing it differently. No, they just don't see the lengths my in-laws are going to. I know what they're doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your belief about your in-laws and the parish committee has been challenged by others who don't see the same pattern that you do, and if so, how do you respond to their different perspective?\n\nPatient: Well, people try to challenge me, sure. My sister tried just the other day. But like I've been saying since I walked in here, this isn't just some silly argument with my in-laws. I know for a fact they are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget every single thing I have done for this family! So when people like my sister tell me I'm seeing patterns that aren't there, I just hang up the phone. \n\nI hung up on her last Tuesday. Or was it Wednesday? It must have been Wednesday because I was watching my daytime stories. I've been watching the same soap opera for thirty years. Although, the storylines lately are just ridiculous. They brought back a character who supposedly died in a plane crash ten years ago! He just walked right into the hospital like nothing happened. I mean, who writes this stuff? My husband used to say those shows would rot my brain, but he would watch football all Sunday, jumping up and yelling at the TV screen like the coach could actually hear him. He spilled a whole bowl of salsa on the living room rug once doing that. I had to scrub it with club soda and baking soda for an hour. Baking soda really is a miracle worker, you know. You can use it for absolutely anything—cleaning, baking, even keeping the fridge smelling fresh. I always keep an open box in the back next to the milk. You have to change it out every thirty days, though, or it stops absorbing the odors. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you ask about my sister?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me whether you have ever questioned your belief about your in-laws and the parish committee, or if you have ever considered that it might not be true?\n\nPatient: Question it? Why would I question it? I came in here today specifically because I knew, I absolutely knew, that my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget everything I have done for this family! It's as clear as day. \n\nJust like the weather was the day of the church picnic last summer. Oh, it was so sunny, not a cloud in the sky. We had it out by the lake, the one with the little wooden docks? I made my famous potato salad. I always use red potatoes because they hold their shape better when you boil them. And a little dash of paprika on top, just for color. My grandmother taught me that trick. She used to grow her own paprika peppers in her garden. Well, I think they were paprika peppers. They were little and red. She had the most beautiful garden, full of roses and hydrangeas. Hydrangeas are so temperamental, though. If the soil is too acidic, they turn blue, and if it's too alkaline, they turn pink. I prefer the blue ones, personally. They remind me of the ocean. I went to the beach once in Florida, Clearwater Beach. The sand was so soft, like powdered sugar. You could walk for miles and it wouldn't hurt your feet at all. \n\nSo no, I haven't ever considered that it might not be true. Why would I?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood was unusually high, irritable, or you felt extremely energetic for at least a week, where you were doing things that were out of character for you?\n\nPatient: High or irritable? Well, yes, I suppose. There were days recently when I felt so bright and... just completely certain about everything. Like I was running on pure electricity. But then, if anyone questioned me about the altar, I would just snap at them like they were insulting my whole family! Just bite their heads right off. \n\nSpeaking of family, my brother-in-law Larry has the absolute worst temper. He got into a screaming match with an umpire at my nephew's Little League game once. Over a strike call! Little League is just too competitive these days. When my son was young, they just played for fun, and we moms would bring orange slices for the dugout. Or wait, do they do orange slices in baseball? Maybe that was soccer. I always got the sports mixed up. But I do know baseball has nine innings, because of the seventh-inning stretch. They always play that song, and people buy peanuts and Cracker Jacks. I never liked peanuts, though. The little skins get stuck right between your teeth, and if you don't have floss handy, it just drives you crazy all afternoon. I always carry a little pack of mint waxed floss in my purse. The unwaxed kind just shreds, especially around the back molars. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Did you ask if I had a mint?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up?\n\nPatient: Well, my sleep has been all over the place. At first, a few weeks ago, I was only sleeping maybe two hours a night. I'd pop right out of bed, full of energy, and start making lists of all the things the parish committee was doing wrong. Especially when we had that old mattress, the one with the pillow top. We bought it at Sears during a Labor Day sale. Sears used to have the best sales. You could get everything there—appliances, clothes, tools. My husband bought his first Craftsman socket set there, and he swore by it. He kept it in this heavy red metal toolbox in the garage. The garage always smelled like motor oil and damp cardboard. We had so many boxes out there. I kept meaning to go through them and organize the Christmas decorations, but you know how it is. You put it off until December, and then you're scrambling to find the good ornaments. I have this one ornament, a little glass bird with a spun-glass tail, that belonged to my mother. You have to clip it onto the branch so carefully or it shatters. Glass is so fragile like that. \n\nWait, did you ask about my mother's ornaments?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when you were involved in activities at a much higher level than usual, like working on multiple projects or being overly involved in church or family affairs?\n\nPatient: Well, I did feel like I could do absolutely everything for a while there. I remember I signed up for all these extra cooking shifts for the church bake sale, and I was trying to organize this whole new group of volunteers. You could stretch it all the way from the kitchen counter into the dining room. It was that mustard yellow color, you know the one? Everyone had those appliances in the seventies. Mustard yellow and avocado green. We had an avocado green refrigerator that lasted twenty years. They just don't make things like they used to. Now everything is stainless steel and it shows every single fingerprint. If you don't use that special spray, it just looks a mess. I buy the spray in bulk at Costco. Costco is wonderful, but you go in for paper towels and you come out with a kayak and a three-pound tub of mayonnaise. Who can eat three pounds of mayonnaise before it goes bad? Unless you're making potato salad for an army, I suppose. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you need a volunteer for something?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when you were involved in activities at a much higher level than usual, like working on multiple projects or being overly involved in church or family affairs?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about the bake sale, yes, absolutely. I just could not stay still for the life of me! I was up cleaning the baseboards, calling people left and right, rewriting all the dinner menus for the month, and dragging heavy boxes of decorations around. My husband and everyone kept telling me to just sit down and rest, but I couldn't! \n\nSpeaking of those decorations, I was getting out the spring things, you know, for Easter. Easter always sneaks up on you when it's in March. I used to make my daughter wear those itchy crinoline slips under her Easter dress so the skirt would puff out. She hated them! Kids' clothes today are so much softer. Everything is that stretchy knit fabric. It's practical, I suppose, but nobody dresses up anymore. You go to church and people are wearing blue jeans! My mother would have had a fit. She never left the house without her hair set and a nice pair of sensible pumps. She bought all her shoes at a little boutique downtown that closed in the nineties. It's a coffee shop now. They charge six dollars for a cup of coffee and they pour the milk into it so it looks like a leaf. It's pretty, but who wants to drink a leaf? And six dollars! I just felt... well, I truly believed I was the only one who could save our family's traditions, you know? Like, if people just listened to me, the whole community event would be absolutely perfect. I had all the answers right there in my head. \n\nIt's like when you have a recipe that's been passed down for generations. My grandmother's pound cake recipe is like that. You have to use real butter, none of that margarine nonsense. Margarine came out and everyone thought it was a miracle, but it's just oil whipped up to look like butter. It doesn't taste the same at all. I remember when they used to deliver milk in glass bottles right to the porch. The cream would rise to the top, and in the winter, if it froze, the little paper cap would pop right off. The milkman's name was Mr. Higgins. He wore a white uniform and drove a truck that you could drive standing up. I always thought that was so strange, driving while standing. What if you hit a bump? You'd go flying right out the door! Cars didn't even have seatbelts back then. We used to ride in the back of my uncle's station wagon, the one with the wood paneling on the side, facing backward and waving at the cars behind us. \n\nWait, did you want the pound cake recipe? I think I have it written down on an index card somewhere.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt unusually energetic or confident for several days in a row, where you needed very little sleep and were doing a lot more than usual, like starting new projects or being very involved in church or family activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I know you keep asking about that. My mother taught me to put a brown paper bag over the wax and run a hot iron over it so the paper absorbs the grease. She had one of those heavy irons, the kind you actually had to be careful with or you'd scorch the fabric right through. People don't iron much anymore. Everything is wrinkle-free this and wash-and-wear that. My husband used to wear a crisp button-down shirt every single day, and I pressed every last one of them with heavy starch. The spray starch in the blue can, Faultless, I think it was called. It smells so clean. I love the smell of clean laundry drying on a line outside in the breeze. You can't do that in my neighborhood anymore, the homeowner's association says it's an eyesore. Can you imagine? A clothesline being an eyesore! They send you a nasty letter if your grass is half an inch too high or if your mailbox is the wrong shade of brown. \n\nWait, what did you ask me just now? Did you get a letter from the association too?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, the sleep. If you try to make a sauce with those, you'll be standing at the stove simmering it for hours just to get it to thicken up. My mother used to simmer her Sunday gravy all day. You have to get the pan screaming hot, sear them quickly on both sides, and take them right off. We had this wonderful waiter named Paul who recommended a crisp white wine to go with it. Well, yes, my thoughts were just flying. My daughter actually got frustrated with me because she said I was talking so fast about all my plans and the family history that she couldn't even get a single word in. But I couldn't stop, I felt like I just had to keep explaining everything right that second before the ideas completely disappeared from my head!

You have to be careful with sponges, though, they hold so much bacteria if you don't microwave them or run them through the wash. I read an article in a magazine at the dentist's office about how the kitchen sponge is the dirtiest thing in the entire house. The dentist I go to has this lovely saltwater tank in the waiting room. Watching the fish swim around is supposed to lower your blood pressure. They have a little yellow tang fish that just darts back and forth all day long, hiding behind the coral. Coral reefs are dying out, which is just a tragedy. I saw a documentary on PBS about it.

I mean, I organized the Easter luncheon for six years straight! I made my famous potato salad—the secret is a little bit of fresh dill, you know. My mother taught me that. She always grew her own dill in this little window box in the kitchen. It faced south, so it got the absolute best morning light. The tomatoes didn't do quite as well, though, because the neighbor's big oak tree blocked the sun by noon. That tree must have been a hundred years old. The city finally cut it down last year, which was a shame, really, all that history just gone through a woodchipper in an afternoon. Makes a terrible racket, too. But... wait, what was I saying? Oh. Yes. The committee. They're trying to erase me.\n\nDoctor: When you say the parish committee is trying to erase you, can you explain exactly what you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Well, it's just like I said, they aren't just arguing with me anymore. It's a whole... a whole orchestrated thing. My in-laws, they're using the altar, the actual altar, and the parish committee to push me out completely. They want to make everyone in the church and the family forget what I have done for them. And I've done so much. Like the time I helped my sister-in-law pick out her wedding dress. We went to this little boutique downtown, the one with the pink awning? They served us champagne in these beautiful crystal flutes. I still have one of them, actually. I kept it as a souvenir. It sits on my mantle right next to the porcelain dog my husband won for me at the state fair in... oh, it must have been 1985. We ate so much cotton candy that day I thought I'd be sick on the Ferris wheel. The lights on that wheel were just spectacular against the night sky, though. You could see them from miles away, just spinning and spinning. But yes, the committee. They're definitely working with my in-laws to erase my whole legacy in this family.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that others might not see things the way you do, or that there could be another explanation for what's happening with your in-laws and the parish committee?\n\nPatient: Another explanation? Well... no, because I can see right through them. Other people might be blind to it, but like I was saying earlier, I know for a fact this isn't just a regular argument. I came in today because I know they are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget everything I've done for this family. \n\nAnd the altar... you know, the wood on that altar is solid mahogany. Father Thomas had it shipped all the way from somewhere in South America, I think Brazil? Or maybe Peru. I had a friend who went to Peru once, Brenda. She brought me back this lovely little woven blanket. It's so soft, made of real alpaca wool. Alpacas are such funny-looking creatures, aren't they? With those long necks and big eyes. They look like they're always judging you. Kind of like my mother-in-law, actually, always looking down her nose when I serve store-bought dinner rolls instead of homemade. But who has the time to wait for yeast to rise when it's so humid out? The humidity just ruins my hair anyway. It gets all frizzy right at the roots, no matter how much hairspray I use. I used to use Aqua Net, the one in the pink can, but they changed the formula and it just isn't the same anymore. \n\nWhat did you ask me? Oh, about other people seeing it differently. No, they just don't see the lengths my in-laws are going to. I know what they're doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your belief about your in-laws and the parish committee has been challenged by others who don't see the same pattern that you do, and if so, how do you respond to their different perspective?\n\nPatient: Well, people try to challenge me, sure. My sister tried just the other day. But like I've been saying since I walked in here, this isn't just some silly argument with my in-laws. I know for a fact they are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget every single thing I have done for this family! So when people like my sister tell me I'm seeing patterns that aren't there, I just hang up the phone. \n\nI hung up on her last Tuesday. Or was it Wednesday? It must have been Wednesday because I was watching my daytime stories. I've been watching the same soap opera for thirty years. Although, the storylines lately are just ridiculous. They brought back a character who supposedly died in a plane crash ten years ago! He just walked right into the hospital like nothing happened. I mean, who writes this stuff? My husband used to say those shows would rot my brain, but he would watch football all Sunday, jumping up and yelling at the TV screen like the coach could actually hear him. He spilled a whole bowl of salsa on the living room rug once doing that. I had to scrub it with club soda and baking soda for an hour. Baking soda really is a miracle worker, you know. You can use it for absolutely anything—cleaning, baking, even keeping the fridge smelling fresh. I always keep an open box in the back next to the milk. You have to change it out every thirty days, though, or it stops absorbing the odors. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you ask about my sister?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me whether you have ever questioned your belief about your in-laws and the parish committee, or if you have ever considered that it might not be true?\n\nPatient: Question it? Why would I question it? I came in here today specifically because I knew, I absolutely knew, that my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget everything I have done for this family! It's as clear as day. \n\nJust like the weather was the day of the church picnic last summer. Oh, it was so sunny, not a cloud in the sky. We had it out by the lake, the one with the little wooden docks? I made my famous potato salad. I always use red potatoes because they hold their shape better when you boil them. And a little dash of paprika on top, just for color. My grandmother taught me that trick. She used to grow her own paprika peppers in her garden. Well, I think they were paprika peppers. They were little and red. She had the most beautiful garden, full of roses and hydrangeas. Hydrangeas are so temperamental, though. If the soil is too acidic, they turn blue, and if it's too alkaline, they turn pink. I prefer the blue ones, personally. They remind me of the ocean. I went to the beach once in Florida, Clearwater Beach. The sand was so soft, like powdered sugar. You could walk for miles and it wouldn't hurt your feet at all. \n\nSo no, I haven't ever considered that it might not be true. Over a strike call! Little League is just too competitive these days. When my son was young, they just played for fun, and we moms would bring orange slices for the dugout. They always play that song, and people buy peanuts and Cracker Jacks. I never liked peanuts, though. The little skins get stuck right between your teeth, and if you don't have floss handy, it just drives you crazy all afternoon. I always carry a little pack of mint waxed floss in my purse. The unwaxed kind just shreds, especially around the back molars. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Did you ask if I had a mint?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up?\n\nPatient: Well, my sleep has been all over the place. At first, a few weeks ago, I was only sleeping maybe two hours a night. I'd pop right out of bed, full of energy, and start making lists of all the things the parish committee was doing wrong. Especially when we had that old mattress, the one with the pillow top. We bought it at Sears during a Labor Day sale. Sears used to have the best sales. You could get everything there—appliances, clothes, tools. My husband bought his first Craftsman socket set there, and he swore by it. He kept it in this heavy red metal toolbox in the garage. The garage always smelled like motor oil and damp cardboard. We had so many boxes out there. I kept meaning to go through them and organize the Christmas decorations, but you know how it is. You put it off until December, and then you're scrambling to find the good ornaments. I have this one ornament, a little glass bird with a spun-glass tail, that belonged to my mother. You have to clip it onto the branch so carefully or it shatters. Glass is so fragile like that. \n\nWait, did you ask about my mother's ornaments?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when you were involved in activities at a much higher level than usual, like working on multiple projects or being overly involved in church or family affairs?\n\nPatient: Well, I did feel like I could do absolutely everything for a while there. At first, I was sleeping only a couple of hours a night, if that, and I'd still be getting up in the middle of the night to make lists. I had lists for absolutely everything. I always use those yellow legal pads for my lists, the ones with the blue lines. But you have to use a good ballpoint pen, none of those gel pens that smear everywhere. My daughter bought me a pack of gel pens once, and I tried to write a grocery list, and by the time I got to the store, my handwriting was just a smudge. I was trying to buy Roma tomatoes for a sauce. You have to use Romas, you know, because the big beefsteak ones have too much water. If you try to make a sauce with those, you'll be standing at the stove simmering it for hours just to get it to thicken up. My mother used to simmer her Sunday gravy all day. We went to a seafood restaurant by the water to celebrate. I had the scallops. I think I have a tin of it in my purse.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, my thoughts were just flying. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I just knew, I mean I was absolutely certain that I was the only person who could save our family's traditions. I felt so capable, you know? Like if everyone would just listen to my plans, the whole community event would be completely perfect. I had it all mapped out in my head. 

I even drew a little diagram on the back of an envelope. It was one of those envelopes from the electric company, the ones with the little plastic window so the address shows through. I don't know why they still mail paper bills, everything is online now. My grandson showed me how to pay my bills on the iPad he got me for Christmas. It has a pink case that folds up into a little stand. Pink was my mother's favorite color, she always wore this pale pink lipstick, I think it was by Revlon. They discontinue all the good colors eventually. I went to the drugstore looking for a lipstick I've worn for years, and the girl behind the counter looked at me like I was speaking a foreign language. She had these very long, sparkly fingernails. I don't know how girls type on their phones with nails like that, it seems like they'd just be clicking all the wrong letters. I tried texting my grandson once and autocorrect changed "soup" to "soap." 

Wait, did you ask me if I brought my iPad today? I think I left it on the kitchen table.

They're definitely working with my in-laws to erase my whole legacy in this family.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that others might not see things the way you do, or that there could be another explanation for what's happening with your in-laws and the parish committee?\n\nPatient: Another explanation? I came in today because I know they are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget everything I've done for this family. \n\nAnd the altar... you know, the wood on that altar is solid mahogany. They look like they're always judging you. Kind of like my mother-in-law, actually, always looking down her nose when I serve store-bought dinner rolls instead of homemade. But who has the time to wait for yeast to rise when it's so humid out? The humidity just ruins my hair anyway. It gets all frizzy right at the roots, no matter how much hairspray I use. I used to use Aqua Net, the one in the pink can, but they changed the formula and it just isn't the same anymore. \n\nWhat did you ask me? Oh, about other people seeing it differently. No, they just don't see the lengths my in-laws are going to. I know what they're doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your belief about your in-laws and the parish committee has been challenged by others who don't see the same pattern that you do, and if so, how do you respond to their different perspective?\n\nPatient: Well, people try to challenge me, sure. My sister tried just the other day. But like I've been saying since I walked in here, this isn't just some silly argument with my in-laws. I know for a fact they are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget every single thing I have done for this family! So when people like my sister tell me I'm seeing patterns that aren't there, I just hang up the phone. \n\nI hung up on her last Tuesday. Or was it Wednesday? It must have been Wednesday because I was watching my daytime stories. I've been watching the same soap opera for thirty years. Although, the storylines lately are just ridiculous. My husband used to say those shows would rot my brain, but he would watch football all Sunday, jumping up and yelling at the TV screen like the coach could actually hear him. Did you ask about my sister?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me whether you have ever questioned your belief about your in-laws and the parish committee, or if you have ever considered that it might not be true?\n\nPatient: Question it? They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget everything I have done for this family! I went to the beach once in Florida, Clearwater Beach. The sand was so soft, like powdered sugar. The little skins get stuck right between your teeth, and if you don't have floss handy, it just drives you crazy all afternoon. I always carry a little pack of mint waxed floss in my purse. Did you ask if I had a mint?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up?\n\nPatient: Well, my sleep has been all over the place. The garage always smelled like motor oil and damp cardboard. We had so many boxes out there. I kept meaning to go through them and organize the Christmas decorations, but you know how it is. You put it off until December, and then you're scrambling to find the good ornaments. I have this one ornament, a little glass bird with a spun-glass tail, that belonged to my mother. You have to clip it onto the branch so carefully or it shatters. You could stretch it all the way from the kitchen counter into the dining room. It was that mustard yellow color, you know the one? Everyone had those appliances in the seventies. Mustard yellow and avocado green. We had an avocado green refrigerator that lasted twenty years. They just don't make things like they used to. Now everything is stainless steel and it shows every single fingerprint. If you don't use that special spray, it just looks a mess. I buy the spray in bulk at Costco. I remember I signed up for all these extra cooking shifts for the church bake sale, and I was trying to organize this whole new group of volunteers. I even started calling up relatives I hadn't spoken to in years, just dialing them up out of the blue because I felt like I had to bring everyone together right that second! \n\nSpeaking of calling people, my old rotary phone used to have the longest cord. You could stretch it all the way from the kitchen counter into the dining room. It was that mustard yellow color, you know the one? Everyone had those appliances in the seventies. Mustard yellow and avocado green. We had an avocado green refrigerator that lasted twenty years. They just don't make things like they used to. Now everything is stainless steel and it shows every single fingerprint. If you don't use that special spray, it just looks a mess. I buy the spray in bulk at Costco. Costco is wonderful, but you go in for paper towels and you come out with a kayak and a three-pound tub of mayonnaise. Who can eat three pounds of mayonnaise before it goes bad? Unless you're making potato salad for an army, I suppose. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you need a volunteer for something?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when you were involved in activities at a much higher level than usual, like working on multiple projects or being overly involved in church or family affairs?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about the bake sale, yes, absolutely. I just could not stay still for the life of me! I was up cleaning the baseboards, calling people left and right, rewriting all the dinner menus for the month, and dragging heavy boxes of decorations around. My husband and everyone kept telling me to just sit down and rest, but I couldn't! \n\nSpeaking of those decorations, I was getting out the spring things, you know, for Easter. Easter always sneaks up on you when it's in March. I used to make my daughter wear those itchy crinoline slips under her Easter dress so the skirt would puff out. She hated them! Kids' clothes today are so much softer. Everything is that stretchy knit fabric. It's practical, I suppose, but nobody dresses up anymore. You go to church and people are wearing blue jeans! My mother would have had a fit. She never left the house without her hair set and a nice pair of sensible pumps. She bought all her shoes at a little boutique downtown that closed in the nineties. It's a coffee shop now. They charge six dollars for a cup of coffee and they pour the milk into it so it looks like a leaf. It's pretty, but who wants to drink a leaf? And six dollars! You used to be able to get a whole diner breakfast for that. Two eggs, bacon, hash browns, and toast. I always liked rye toast with the little caraway seeds. \n\nWait, what did you just ask me? Did you want to know what I had for breakfast?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when you were involved in activities at a much higher level than usual, like working on multiple projects or being overly involved in church or family affairs?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose so. I just felt... well, I truly believed I was the only one who could save our family's traditions, you know? Like, if people just listened to me, the whole community event would be absolutely perfect. I had all the answers right there in my head. \n\nIt's like when you have a recipe that's been passed down for generations. My grandmother's pound cake recipe is like that. You have to use real butter, none of that margarine nonsense. Margarine came out and everyone thought it was a miracle, but it's just oil whipped up to look like butter. It doesn't taste the same at all. I remember when they used to deliver milk in glass bottles right to the porch. The cream would rise to the top, and in the winter, if it froze, the little paper cap would pop right off. The milkman's name was Mr. Higgins. He wore a white uniform and drove a truck that you could drive standing up. I always thought that was so strange, driving while standing. What if you hit a bump? You'd go flying right out the door! Cars didn't even have seatbelts back then. We used to ride in the back of my uncle's station wagon, the one with the wood paneling on the side, facing backward and waving at the cars behind us. \n\nWait, did you want the pound cake recipe? I think I have it written down on an index card somewhere.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt unusually energetic or confident for several days in a row, where you needed very little sleep and were doing a lot more than usual, like starting new projects or being very involved in church or family activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I know you keep asking about that. There were days when I felt just so bright and certain about everything. I was working on the church altar, and I had it all perfectly planned out in my head. But then, if anyone even questioned me about the altar, I would just snap at them like they were insulting my whole family! I had zero patience for anyone interfering. \n\nSpeaking of the altar, we always used those tall beeswax candles. Real beeswax, mind you, not the cheap paraffin ones that drip everywhere and ruin the good lace linens. Getting wax out of lace is an absolute nightmare. My mother taught me to put a brown paper bag over the wax and run a hot iron over it so the paper absorbs the grease. She had one of those heavy irons, the kind you actually had to be careful with or you'd scorch the fabric right through. People don't iron much anymore. Everything is wrinkle-free this and wash-and-wear that. My husband used to wear a crisp button-down shirt every single day, and I pressed every last one of them with heavy starch. The spray starch in the blue can, Faultless, I think it was called. It smells so clean. I love the smell of clean laundry drying on a line outside in the breeze. You can't do that in my neighborhood anymore, the homeowner's association says it's an eyesore. Can you imagine? A clothesline being an eyesore! They send you a nasty letter if your grass is half an inch too high or if your mailbox is the wrong shade of brown. \n\nWait, what did you ask me just now? Did you get a letter from the association too?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, the sleep. My daughter bought me a pack of gel pens once, and I tried to write a grocery list, and by the time I got to the store, my handwriting was just a smudge. I was trying to buy Roma tomatoes for a sauce. You have to use Romas, you know, because the big beefsteak ones have too much water. If you try to make a sauce with those, you'll be standing at the stove simmering it for hours just to get it to thicken up. My mother used to simmer her Sunday gravy all day. The whole house smelled like garlic and basil. We grew basil in a little pot right on the kitchen windowsill. \n\nBut anyway, it's not like that anymore. Now I just lie awake before dawn worrying about every little thing, and I never, ever feel rested. Being awake before dawn is just awful because that's when the birds start up. They'll dive-bomb a cat if it gets too close to their nest. We had an orange tabby cat named Marmalade when I was a girl, and he was absolutely terrified of the birds. He used to hide under the front porch whenever the blue jays came around. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, yes, absolutely. I had so much energy I just couldn't sit still. I signed up for all these extra cooking shifts at the parish, and I was trying to organize all the new volunteers, and I was even calling relatives I hadn't spoken to in years because I just felt like I had to bring everyone together right away! 

I was making so many casseroles for the church. I always use my vintage Pyrex for casseroles, the ones with the little green daisies on the side. They call that pattern Spring Blossom. You just can't find good glass like that in regular stores anymore, you have to go hunting at estate sales. My friend Linda and I used to go to estate sales every Sunday. She collected those little ceramic owls from the 1970s. Owls are supposed to be very wise, you know, but they can be quite aggressive if you get too near their nests. My uncle had a barn owl living in his hayloft out in Ohio. Ohio gets so terribly cold in the winter, the snow just piles up right to the bottom of the windowsills. You really have to have a good snow shovel, the ergonomic kind with the bent handle, otherwise you'll just ruin your lower back. Back pain is miserable. My husband had sciatica for years, he had to sleep with a special pillow between his knees just to get comfortable. 

Wait, what were we just talking about? Did you ask me if I have back pain? I think this chair is a little stiff.

Well, yes, there were days when I just felt so completely bright and certain about absolutely everything. I was setting up the altar at the church, and I knew exactly how it needed to be done. But then if anyone even questioned me about the altar or tried to move a single flower, I would just snap at them like they were insulting my whole family! 

I was using those tall white taper candles, you see. You really have to get the dripless kind, otherwise the wax gets all over the good linen altar cloths. Getting wax out of linen is just a complete nightmare. You have to put a brown paper bag over the spot and run a warm iron over it so the paper absorbs the wax. I learned that trick from my old neighbor, Mrs. Gable. She used to iron absolutely everything, even her husband's underwear and her pillowcases. I don't know who has the time to iron pillowcases, especially since most fabrics are wrinkle-free now anyway. They put all sorts of chemicals in the cotton these days to make it like that. Cotton just isn't what it used to be. My husband used to wear those thick cotton undershirts, and they would shrink right up to nothing in the dryer if you weren't careful. Dryers use so much electricity anyway, I always try to hang my sheets on the line in the backyard when the weather is nice. The birds can be a terrible nuisance, though, especially the blue jays. They are so loud in the mornings, just squawking and chasing the smaller birds away from the suet feeder. 

Wait, I'm sorry, what were we just talking about? Did you ask me if the birds were waking me up early?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, sleep has been very strange lately. At first, I was sleeping only a couple of hours a night, and I would just pop right out of bed in the dark to make lists for all the things I was going to do. I always make my lists on those yellow legal pads, the ones with the perforated tops so you can tear the sheet off cleanly. You have to use a good pen, though, like one of those smooth gel pens. I prefer blue ink. Black ink always looks so severe, like a notice from the IRS or a parking ticket. My brother-in-law got audited by the IRS back in the nineties, and he had to bring in literal shoeboxes full of receipts to their office. Who keeps receipts in shoeboxes anymore? Shoe boxes aren't even made as well as they used to be anyway. The cardboard is so flimsy now. I bought a pair of loafers last spring, just sensible navy blue ones for walking, and the box practically fell apart on the way to the car. You really need good arch support when you get to my age, otherwise your knees take all the impact on the pavement. My neighbor had both her knees replaced last year, and she had to do all her physical therapy in a swimming pool. Chlorine always dries out my skin so terribly, I have to use that thick Eucerin cream. 

Wait, what did you just ask me? Oh, right, my sleep. Yes, so I was making all those lists on very little sleep, but now... now it's completely different. Now I just lie awake for hours before dawn, just tossing and turning and worrying about absolutely everything, and I just never feel rested at all when morning comes. Do you think I need a new mattress? The one I have has springs, and I heard everyone is buying that memory foam stuff now.

The tomatoes didn't do quite as well, though, because the neighbor's big oak tree blocked the sun by noon. That tree must have been a hundred years old. They look like they're always judging you. Kind of like my mother-in-law, actually, always looking down her nose when I serve store-bought dinner rolls instead of homemade. But who has the time to wait for yeast to rise when it's so humid out? The humidity just ruins my hair anyway. It gets all frizzy right at the roots, no matter how much hairspray I use. I used to use Aqua Net, the one in the pink can, but they changed the formula and it just isn't the same anymore. \n\nWhat did you ask me? Oh, about other people seeing it differently. No, they just don't see the lengths my in-laws are going to. I know what they're doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your belief about your in-laws and the parish committee has been challenged by others who don't see the same pattern that you do, and if so, how do you respond to their different perspective?\n\nPatient: Well, people try to challenge me, sure. My sister tried just the other day. But like I've been saying since I walked in here, this isn't just some silly argument with my in-laws. I know for a fact they are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget every single thing I have done for this family! So when people like my sister tell me I'm seeing patterns that aren't there, I just hang up the phone. \n\nI hung up on her last Tuesday. Or was it Wednesday? It must have been Wednesday because I was watching my daytime stories. I've been watching the same soap opera for thirty years. Although, the storylines lately are just ridiculous. They brought back a character who supposedly died in a plane crash ten years ago! He just walked right into the hospital like nothing happened. I mean, who writes this stuff? My husband used to say those shows would rot my brain, but he would watch football all Sunday, jumping up and yelling at the TV screen like the coach could actually hear him. He spilled a whole bowl of salsa on the living room rug once doing that. I had to scrub it with club soda and baking soda for an hour. Baking soda really is a miracle worker, you know. You can use it for absolutely anything—cleaning, baking, even keeping the fridge smelling fresh. I always keep an open box in the back next to the milk. You have to change it out every thirty days, though, or it stops absorbing the odors. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you ask about my sister?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me whether you have ever questioned your belief about your in-laws and the parish committee, or if you have ever considered that it might not be true?\n\nPatient: Question it? Why would I question it? I came in here today specifically because I knew, I absolutely knew, that my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget everything I have done for this family! It's as clear as day. \n\nJust like the weather was the day of the church picnic last summer. Oh, it was so sunny, not a cloud in the sky. We had it out by the lake, the one with the little wooden docks? I made my famous potato salad. I always use red potatoes because they hold their shape better when you boil them. And a little dash of paprika on top, just for color. My grandmother taught me that trick. She used to grow her own paprika peppers in her garden. Well, I think they were paprika peppers. They were little and red. She had the most beautiful garden, full of roses and hydrangeas. Hydrangeas are so temperamental, though. If the soil is too acidic, they turn blue, and if it's too alkaline, they turn pink. I prefer the blue ones, personally. They remind me of the ocean. I went to the beach once in Florida, Clearwater Beach. The sand was so soft, like powdered sugar. You could walk for miles and it wouldn't hurt your feet at all. \n\nSo no, I haven't ever considered that it might not be true. Why would I?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood was unusually high, irritable, or you felt extremely energetic for at least a week, where you were doing things that were out of character for you?\n\nPatient: High or irritable? Well, yes, I suppose. There were days recently when I felt so bright and... just completely certain about everything. Like I was running on pure electricity. But then, if anyone questioned me about the altar, I would just snap at them like they were insulting my whole family! Just bite their heads right off. \n\nSpeaking of family, my brother-in-law Larry has the absolute worst temper. He got into a screaming match with an umpire at my nephew's Little League game once. Over a strike call! Little League is just too competitive these days. When my son was young, they just played for fun, and we moms would bring orange slices for the dugout. Or wait, do they do orange slices in baseball? Maybe that was soccer. I always got the sports mixed up. But I do know baseball has nine innings, because of the seventh-inning stretch. They always play that song, and people buy peanuts and Cracker Jacks. I never liked peanuts, though. The little skins get stuck right between your teeth, and if you don't have floss handy, it just drives you crazy all afternoon. I always carry a little pack of mint waxed floss in my purse. The unwaxed kind just shreds, especially around the back molars. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Did you ask if I had a mint?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up?\n\nPatient: Well, my sleep has been all over the place. At first, a few weeks ago, I was only sleeping maybe two hours a night. I'd pop right out of bed, full of energy, and start making lists of all the things the parish committee was doing wrong. But lately, it's different. Now I just lie awake in the dark, hours before dawn, just worrying and worrying, and I never feel rested anymore. \n\nI used to sleep like a rock, though. Especially when we had that old mattress, the one with the pillow top. We bought it at Sears during a Labor Day sale. Sears used to have the best sales. You could get everything there—appliances, clothes, tools. My husband bought his first Craftsman socket set there, and he swore by it. He kept it in this heavy red metal toolbox in the garage. The garage always smelled like motor oil and damp cardboard. We had so many boxes out there. I kept meaning to go through them and organize the Christmas decorations, but you know how it is. You put it off until December, and then you're scrambling to find the good ornaments. I have this one ornament, a little glass bird with a spun-glass tail, that belonged to my mother. You have to clip it onto the branch so carefully or it shatters. Glass is so fragile like that. \n\nWait, did you ask about my mother's ornaments?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when you were involved in activities at a much higher level than usual, like working on multiple projects or being overly involved in church or family affairs?\n\nPatient: Well, I did feel like I could do absolutely everything for a while there. I remember I signed up for all these extra cooking shifts for the church bake sale, and I was trying to organize this whole new group of volunteers. I even started calling up relatives I hadn't spoken to in years, just dialing them up out of the blue because I felt like I had to bring everyone together right that second! \n\nSpeaking of calling people, my old rotary phone used to have the longest cord. You could stretch it all the way from the kitchen counter into the dining room. It was that mustard yellow color, you know the one? Everyone had those appliances in the seventies. Mustard yellow and avocado green. We had an avocado green refrigerator that lasted twenty years. They just don't make things like they used to. Now everything is stainless steel and it shows every single fingerprint. If you don't use that special spray, it just looks a mess. I buy the spray in bulk at Costco. Costco is wonderful, but you go in for paper towels and you come out with a kayak and a three-pound tub of mayonnaise. Who can eat three pounds of mayonnaise before it goes bad? Unless you're making potato salad for an army, I suppose. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you need a volunteer for something?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when you were involved in activities at a much higher level than usual, like working on multiple projects or being overly involved in church or family affairs?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying about the bake sale, yes, absolutely. I just could not stay still for the life of me! I was up cleaning the baseboards, calling people left and right, rewriting all the dinner menus for the month, and dragging heavy boxes of decorations around. My husband and everyone kept telling me to just sit down and rest, but I couldn't! \n\nSpeaking of those decorations, I was getting out the spring things, you know, for Easter. Easter always sneaks up on you when it's in March. I used to make my daughter wear those itchy crinoline slips under her Easter dress so the skirt would puff out. She hated them! Kids' clothes today are so much softer. Everything is that stretchy knit fabric. It's practical, I suppose, but nobody dresses up anymore. You go to church and people are wearing blue jeans! My mother would have had a fit. She never left the house without her hair set and a nice pair of sensible pumps. She bought all her shoes at a little boutique downtown that closed in the nineties. It's a coffee shop now. They charge six dollars for a cup of coffee and they pour the milk into it so it looks like a leaf. It's pretty, but who wants to drink a leaf? And six dollars! You used to be able to get a whole diner breakfast for that. Two eggs, bacon, hash browns, and toast. I always liked rye toast with the little caraway seeds. \n\nWait, what did you just ask me? Did you want to know what I had for breakfast?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when you were involved in activities at a much higher level than usual, like working on multiple projects or being overly involved in church or family affairs?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose so. I just felt... well, I truly believed I was the only one who could save our family's traditions, you know? Like, if people just listened to me, the whole community event would be absolutely perfect. I had all the answers right there in my head. \n\nIt's like when you have a recipe that's been passed down for generations. My grandmother's pound cake recipe is like that. You have to use real butter, none of that margarine nonsense. Margarine came out and everyone thought it was a miracle, but it's just oil whipped up to look like butter. It doesn't taste the same at all. I remember when they used to deliver milk in glass bottles right to the porch. The cream would rise to the top, and in the winter, if it froze, the little paper cap would pop right off. The milkman's name was Mr. Higgins. He wore a white uniform and drove a truck that you could drive standing up. I always thought that was so strange, driving while standing. What if you hit a bump? You'd go flying right out the door! Cars didn't even have seatbelts back then. We used to ride in the back of my uncle's station wagon, the one with the wood paneling on the side, facing backward and waving at the cars behind us. \n\nWait, did you want the pound cake recipe? Real beeswax, mind you, not the cheap paraffin ones that drip everywhere and ruin the good lace linens. Getting wax out of lace is an absolute nightmare. My mother taught me to put a brown paper bag over the wax and run a hot iron over it so the paper absorbs the grease. She had one of those heavy irons, the kind you actually had to be careful with or you'd scorch the fabric right through. People don't iron much anymore. Everything is wrinkle-free this and wash-and-wear that. My husband used to wear a crisp button-down shirt every single day, and I pressed every last one of them with heavy starch. The spray starch in the blue can, Faultless, I think it was called. It smells so clean. I love the smell of clean laundry drying on a line outside in the breeze. You can't do that in my neighborhood anymore, the homeowner's association says it's an eyesore. Can you imagine? A clothesline being an eyesore! They send you a nasty letter if your grass is half an inch too high or if your mailbox is the wrong shade of brown. \n\nWait, what did you ask me just now? Did you get a letter from the association too?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time when you felt so energetic or confident that you didn’t need much sleep, or when your thoughts were racing so fast you couldn’t keep up?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, the sleep. I was trying to buy Roma tomatoes for a sauce. You have to use Romas, you know, because the big beefsteak ones have too much water. If you try to make a sauce with those, you'll be standing at the stove simmering it for hours just to get it to thicken up. My mother used to simmer her Sunday gravy all day. They'll dive-bomb a cat if it gets too close to their nest. We had an orange tabby cat named Marmalade when I was a girl, and he was absolutely terrified of the birds. He used to hide under the front porch whenever the blue jays came around. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Did you want to know if I have a cat?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your belief about your in-laws and the parish committee trying to erase you is something you feel absolutely certain about, even if others say it isn’t true or there’s no evidence for it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely certain.
